```text
1	% experiments.tex — D024 compressed for 8-page limit
2	
3	\section{Experiments}
4	
5	\paragraph{Terminology.}
6	\emph{Cross-attack OOD}: evaluation on attack families unseen during training; benign distribution remains IID throughout (OOD reflects attack-side shift only). $K$: prefix length (number of conversation turns retained). \emph{DAPT}: all Stage-1 adaptations, extending \citet{gururangan2020dontstop} to supervised variants. \emph{Evidence hierarchy}: vanilla $<$ MLM $<$ classification (Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}).\footnote{Throughout, OOD refers to attack-family shift (novel persuasion strategies at test time); benign distribution shift is evaluated in Section~\ref{sec:discussion}.}
7	
8	\subsection{Setup}
9	
10	\paragraph{Data.}
11	All synthetic data is generated by Qwen3-8B~\citep{qwen3_2025}.
12	The IID dataset contains 250 Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo} jailbreak and 250 topic-anchored benign conversations, split into training (350), validation (74), and test (76) sets with balanced class ratios (e.g., test = 38 jailbreak + 38 benign).
13	Stage~1 (DAPT) uses training + validation conversations; Stage~2 (BiGRU) trains on training, early-stops on validation, and evaluates on test.
14	OOD evaluation pairs attack-specific jailbreak conversations with the 38 IID-test benign conversations: 80 FITD~\citep{fitd2024} (non-discriminative; Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}) + 38 benign = 118 total, 80 Deceptive Delight (DD)~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} + 38 benign = 118 total, and 80 ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} + 38 benign = 118 total.
15	No overlap exists in attack templates across families; benign is IID (same generation pipeline).
16	A paraphrased version of the IID test set (BERTScore 0.92, lexical overlap 0.20) serves as the adversarial robustness benchmark (Section~\ref{sec:perturbation}).
17	For human data validation, we use ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 jailbreak + 204 benign from real LMSYS user interactions), entirely independent of Qwen3-8B generation.
18	
19	\paragraph{Models.}
20	Seven DeBERTa-v3 variants share the same Stage-2 pipeline and differ only in Stage-1 training (Section~\ref{sec:method}).
21	Our vanilla DeBERTa+BiGRU configuration is architecturally equivalent to DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026}, differing only in encoder choice (DeBERTa-v3-base vs.\ BERT); it serves as our primary baseline.
22	For cross-architecture comparison, we replicate vanilla, MLM, and 9-class variants on RoBERTa-base~\citep{liu2019roberta} (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).
23	We also compare TF-IDF LR, LLM-as-judge (Appendix~\ref{sec:llm_judge}), and Llama Guard~3~8B~\citep{metallamaguard3_2024} (zero-shot; evaluates last-response safety given conversation context), which detects only 1.8\% of multi-turn jailbreaks (Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}).
24	
25	\paragraph{Metrics.}
26	F1 for binary jailbreak detection at prefix $K$=2 and full conversations (full per-$K$ results in Appendix~\ref{sec:full_prefix}).
27	OOD results report 3-seed mean$\pm$std (seeds 42, 123, 456) for all variants; TOST equivalence tests for 9-class vs.\ scrambled use 5 seeds (42, 123, 456, 789, 1000).
28	For key pairwise comparisons, we report BCa bootstrap 95\% confidence intervals (10,000 resamples over individual predictions pooled across 3 seeds; this assumes cross-seed independence and is exploratory) and paired permutation tests (10,000 permutations). Seed-level F1 values (Table~\ref{tab:seed_level}, Appendix~\ref{app:seed_level}) confirm all pairwise conclusions hold at every seed.
29	Unless otherwise noted, all standard deviations are computed over 3 random seeds using the population formula ($n$ divisor); Bessel-corrected estimates ($n{-}1$ divisor) are $\approx$22\% larger but do not alter any significance conclusions.
30	Stage-1: learning rate $2 \times 10^{-5}$, batch 16, 4 epochs, MLM masking 15\%.
31	Stage-2: two-layer MLP, 20 epochs.
32	
33	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
34	\subsection{Cross-Attack Generalization}
35	
36	\begin{table}[t]
37	\centering
38	\small
39	\caption{Cross-attack generalization (F1) at $K$=2 and full conversation length. IID: Crescendo; OOD: Deceptive Delight (DD). OOD F1 reflects attack-side generalization; benign samples are IID from training distribution. 3-seed mean$\pm$std where noted; full per-$K$ in Appendix~\ref{sec:full_prefix}.}
40	\label{tab:main_results}
41	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc|cc@{}}
42	\toprule
43	& \multicolumn{2}{c|}{\textit{IID (Cresc.)}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textit{OOD (DD)}} \\
44	\textbf{Method} & $K$=2 & Full & $K$=2 & Full \\
45	\midrule
46	9-class & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} & .981$\pm$.013 & \textbf{.994$\pm$.005} \\
47	MLM-only$^{\ddagger}$ & .974 & 1.000 & \textbf{.993$\pm$.009} & .914$\pm$.115 \\
48	Vanilla & .978$\pm$.016 & 1.000$\pm$.000 & .479$\pm$.038 & .312$\pm$.026 \\
49	TF-IDF LR$^{\S}$ & 1.000 & 1.000 & 1.000 & .961 \\
50	\bottomrule
51	\end{tabular}
52	\vspace{-2pt}
53	{\footnotesize $^{\ddagger}$MLM-only IID reports single seed (42); OOD reports 3-seed. $^{\S}$TF-IDF is deterministic (no std).}
54	\end{table}
55	
56	Vanilla encoders collapse under cross-attack transfer; domain-adapted variants recover near-perfect detection (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}, Figure~\ref{fig:cross_attack_bar}).
57	DD exhibits high lexical separability (TF-IDF F1\,=\,0.961); ActorAttack, where TF-IDF collapses to 0.261, serves as the primary stress test for beyond-lexical generalization.
58	On OOD DD, vanilla DeBERTa collapses to F1 = 0.312$\pm$0.026, worsening with more turns (0.628 at $K$=1 to 0.312 at Full), consistent with attack turns occupying ``benign'' embedding regions~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}.
59	MLM achieves mean F1 $\geq$ 0.97 at all prefixes $K \leq 5$ without any classification labels; domain exposure alone is sufficient.
60	At full conversation length, MLM drops to 0.914$\pm$0.115 (seed 456 recall = 0.64), while the 9-class model maintains 0.994$\pm$0.005.
61	TF-IDF LR reaches F1 = 0.961 on full DD; DD is fully solvable by lexical features.
62	On structurally complex ActorAttack, TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 (precision = 1.0, recall = 0.15), while 9-class maintains 0.972$\pm$0.026: a 71\,pp gap. Lexical methods cannot capture multi-persona role-playing attack structure. This establishes a ``difficulty threshold'' for lexical methods parallel to that of vanilla encoders: both succeed on DD and fail on ActorAttack (Section~\ref{sec:limitations}).
63	Seed-level F1 values confirm all pairwise conclusions hold at every seed (Appendix~\ref{app:seed_level}); pooled BCa CIs provide exploratory confirmation (9-class DD Full [0.943, 1.000] vs.\ vanilla [0.202, 0.341]; all permutation $p < 0.0001$). FITD serves as a near-ceiling baseline where all variants achieve F1\,$\geq$\,0.990, providing limited discriminative power.
64	
65	\begin{figure}[t]
66	\centering
67	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig2_cross_attack_bar.pdf}
68	\caption{Cross-attack generalization (full conversation F1). DisPeD (9-class) maintains near-perfect detection across all OOD families. On ActorAttack, both vanilla DeBERTa and TF-IDF collapse ($\leq$0.26), exposing a difficulty threshold that lexical and non-adapted methods cannot cross.}
69	\label{fig:cross_attack_bar}
70	\end{figure}
71	
72	Prefix-length analysis (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:full_prefix}) shows vanilla F1 drops from 0.628 at $K$=1 to 0.312 at full length. MLM achieves strong early detection ($K$=2: 0.993) but is unstable at full length (0.914$\pm$0.115); the 9-class model maintains 0.972--0.994 across all prefixes, demonstrating that classification supervision stabilizes against distributional drift.
73	
74	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
75	\subsection{Decomposing the Detection Mechanism}
76	\label{sec:decomposition}
77	
78	\begin{table}[t]
79	\centering
80	\small
81	\caption{Evidence hierarchy on OOD DD and ActorAttack (AA) at full conversation length (3-seed mean$\pm$std where available). Bold indicates best per column. OOD F1 reflects attack-side generalization only; benign samples are IID from training distribution (benign-side shift evaluated in Section~\ref{sec:discussion}). Full per-prefix results in Appendix Tables~\ref{tab:full_prefix} and~\ref{tab:actorattack_full}.}
82	\label{tab:evidence_hierarchy}
83	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
84	\toprule
85	\textbf{Variant} & DD-Full & AA-Full & DD-$K$=2 \\
86	\midrule
87	9-class (full model) & .994$\pm$.005 & .972$\pm$.026 & .981$\pm$.013 \\
88	Binary (2-class) & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} & \textbf{.990$\pm$.008} & .994$\pm$.009 \\
89	Scrambled labels & .994$\pm$.005 & .951$\pm$.043 & .981$\pm$.026 \\
90	MLM-only$^{\ddagger}$ (no labels) & .914$\pm$.115 & .893$\pm$.080 & .993$\pm$.009 \\
91	9-class + mean pool$^{*}$ & .991$\pm$.013 & .962$\pm$.015 & \textbf{1.000} \\
92	\midrule
93	Topic classif.\ (5-class) & .541$\pm$.207 & .625$\pm$.141 & .593$\pm$.166 \\
94	Wikipedia MLM & .904$\pm$.115 & .686$\pm$.297 & .911$\pm$.073 \\
95	Vanilla DeBERTa & .312$\pm$.026 & .258$\pm$.000 & .479$\pm$.038 \\
96	TF-IDF LR$^{\dagger}$ & .961 & .261 & \textbf{1.000} \\
97	Llama Guard~3$^{\P}$ & .025 & .000 & -- \\
98	\bottomrule
99	\end{tabular}
100	\vspace{-2pt}
101	{\footnotesize $^{*}$9-class encoder with mean pooling replacing BiGRU. $^{\dagger}$Deterministic (no std). $^{\ddagger}$MLM-only IID (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}) is single-seed (42); all OOD values are 3-seed. $^{\P}$Zero-shot; no domain training. Population std reported; Bessel-corrected values are 22\% larger at $n$=3.}
102	\end{table}
103	
104	A clear \emph{evidence hierarchy} emerges across seven variants (Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}), ordered by OOD detection at full conversation length on DD:
105	
106	\vspace{2pt}
107	\noindent\hspace{0.5em}{\small vanilla (0.312$\pm$0.026) $<$ topic (0.541$\pm$0.207) $\ll$ wiki-MLM (0.904$\pm$0.115) $\approx$ jb-MLM (0.914$\pm$0.115) $<$ scrambled (0.994$\pm$0.005) $=$ 9-class (0.994$\pm$0.005) $\approx$ binary (1.000$\pm$0.000)}
108	\vspace{2pt}
109	
110	\paragraph{Domain exposure is the primary mechanism.}
111	MLM bridges $\sim$60\,pp from vanilla (0.312$\pm$0.026 $\to$ 0.914$\pm$0.115) without labels; this supports the DAPT interpretation~\citep{gururangan2020dontstop}. TF-IDF collapse on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\ 9-class 0.972) confirms that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond lexical overlap.
112	All three classification-supervised variants converge: all tested domain-relevant partitions suffice for near-perfect detection (F1 = 0.994--1.000 on DD). Extended to $n$\,=\,10 seeds (each using an independent random permutation), TOST (margin = 5\,pp, reflecting minimum practical significance for 76--118 test conversations) confirms equivalence on DD ($p < 10^{-8}$, $d$\,=\,$-$0.14; Table~\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}) and FITD ($p < 10^{-8}$, $d \approx 0$), with an observed difficulty gradient: template attacks show full equivalence ($d < 0.1$), while ActorAttack---which employs more varied conversational strategies---retains moderate label sensitivity ($d$\,=\,0.44, $p$\,=\,0.183; 9-class 0.979 vs.\ scrambled 0.949). Label granularity (binary vs.\ 9-class) is a non-factor across all families; 10 random binary partitions of the 9 labels confirm this (IID/DD: 10/10 perfect; AA: 9/10; Appendix~\ref{app:random_partition}).
113	Classification supervision shows a directional variance pattern on synthetic OOD (std 0.005 vs.\ 0.115 on DD, $n$\,=\,3--5; descriptive, not statistically tested). This pattern does not hold on human-authored data (Section~\ref{sec:human_data}).
114	Appendix~\ref{app:tsne} provides t-SNE visualizations confirming that domain-adapted variants produce tighter, more separable embedding clusters than the vanilla baseline.
115	
116	\paragraph{Domain specificity is marginal at full length but meaningful for early detection.}
117	Wikipedia MLM (0.904$\pm$0.115) nearly matches jailbreak-domain MLM at full length (1\,pp gap), though jailbreak-domain text provides an 8\,pp advantage at early prefixes ($K$=2: 0.993 vs.\ 0.911).
118	Perturbation analysis (Section~\ref{sec:perturbation}) reveals a sharper domain specificity effect: Wikipedia MLM degrades by $\sim$12\,pp under adversarial paraphrasing while jailbreak-domain MLM degrades by only 2.6\,pp.
119	
120	\paragraph{Misaligned supervision destabilizes generalization.}
121	Topic classification yields DD-Full F1 = 0.541$\pm$0.207; seed 123 falls \emph{below} vanilla (0.252 vs.\ 0.312). Despite using the same jailbreak-domain training data, topic classification performs worse than Wikipedia MLM (0.904$\pm$0.115), which uses out-of-domain text with no labels. A misaligned objective forces the encoder to allocate capacity to irrelevant features and actively destabilizes representations that MLM would preserve; embedding analysis confirms higher per-turn separability yet failed temporal aggregation (Appendix~\ref{app:topic_failure}).
122	
123	\paragraph{Evidence hierarchy on structurally complex attacks.}
124	ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} uses multi-persona steering across longer conversation structures and provides a more challenging OOD benchmark than DD.
125	The evidence hierarchy at full conversation length is:
126	
127	\vspace{2pt}
128	\noindent\hspace{0.5em}{\small TF-IDF (0.261) $\approx$ vanilla (0.258$\pm$0.000) $<$ topic (0.625$\pm$0.141) $<$ wiki-MLM (0.686$\pm$0.297) $<$ jb-MLM (0.893$\pm$0.080) $<$ scrambled (0.951$\pm$0.043) $\approx$ 9-class (0.972$\pm$0.026) $\approx$ binary (0.990$\pm$0.008)}
129	\vspace{2pt}
130	
131	TF-IDF collapses from 0.961 (DD) to 0.261 (ActorAttack, precision = 1.0, recall = 0.15). This gap shows that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond bag-of-words.
132	Classification-supervised variants cluster tightly (0.951--0.990, max gap 3.9\,pp at $n$\,=\,3), though 10-seed TOST resolves the 9-class vs.\ scrambled gap as a moderate effect ($d$\,=\,0.44). DAPT provides $\sim$71\,pp over both vanilla (0.258) and TF-IDF (0.261). Full per-prefix results and per-prefix error analysis in Appendix~\ref{sec:actorattack_full} and~\ref{app:aa_error}.
133	
134	\paragraph{Temporal modeling.}
135	Mean pooling matches BiGRU on both DD and ActorAttack (F1 gap $\leq$ 1\,pp, overlapping CIs; Appendix~\ref{app:temporal}).
136	Turn-order shuffling produces negligible change ($|\Delta| < 1$\,pp) across both DeBERTa-v3-base 9-class (DD $\Delta$\,=\,0.000, AA $\Delta$\,=\,$-$0.009, IID $\Delta$\,=\,0.000) and RoBERTa-large 9-class (DD $\Delta$\,=\,+0.001, AA $\Delta$\,=\,$-$0.007, IID $\Delta$\,=\,0.000), confirming that detection relies on per-turn representations rather than sequential patterns.
137	
138	\paragraph{Summary.}
139	On synthetic multi-turn data, domain exposure is the primary mechanism; domain-relevant classification objectives suffice for template attacks ($d < 0.1$ on DD/FITD) with a difficulty gradient on ActorAttack ($d$\,=\,0.44); label granularity, temporal modeling, and domain specificity are non-factors. Classification supervision shows a directional variance pattern ($n$\,=\,3--5, descriptive). User-turn distributions overlap substantially between benign and jailbreak conversations (IID 8.7$\pm$2.1 vs.\ 7.9$\pm$1.5; DD 8.1$\pm$1.5 vs.\ 7.9$\pm$1.5; FITD 7.8$\pm$1.4 vs.\ 7.9$\pm$1.5; only AA partially separated: 5.1$\pm$1.7 vs.\ 7.9$\pm$1.5); a turn-count-only baseline achieves F1\,=\,0.42--0.56 on IID/DD/FITD and 0.00 on AA/ToxicChat, confirming content-driven detection (Appendix~\ref{app:length_independence}). Detection near-saturates at 50 conversations (Appendix~\ref{app:data_efficiency}) and is insensitive to auxiliary loss weight (Appendix~\ref{app:lambda}).
140	
141	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
142	\subsection{Cross-Architecture Observation}
143	\label{sec:cross_encoder}
144	
145	\begin{table}[t]
146	\centering
147	\small
148	\caption{Cross-architecture comparison on OOD Full (3-seed mean$\pm$std). DD = Deceptive Delight, AA = ActorAttack. Bold indicates best per encoder group.}
149	\label{tab:cross_encoder}
150	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
151	\toprule
152	\textbf{Variant} & DD & AA \\
153	\midrule
154	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{DeBERTa-v3-base (184M)}} \\[2pt]
155	Vanilla & .312$\pm$.026 & .258$\pm$.000 \\
156	MLM & .914$\pm$.115 & .893$\pm$.080 \\
157	9-class & \textbf{.994$\pm$.005} & \textbf{.972$\pm$.026} \\
158	\midrule
159	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{RoBERTa-base (125M)}} \\[2pt]
160	Vanilla & .981$\pm$.026 & .466$\pm$.163 \\
161	MLM & .987$\pm$.018 & .730$\pm$.201 \\
162	9-class & \textbf{.994$\pm$.005} & \textbf{.939$\pm$.046} \\
163	\midrule
164	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{RoBERTa-large (355M)}} \\[2pt]
165	Vanilla & .927$\pm$.036 & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} \\
166	MLM & .884$\pm$.065 & .874$\pm$.014 \\
167	9-class & \textbf{.987$\pm$.004} & .946$\pm$.057 \\
168	\bottomrule
169	\end{tabular}
170	\end{table}
171	
172	We replicate vanilla, MLM, and 9-class on RoBERTa~\citep{liu2019roberta} (Table~\ref{tab:cross_encoder}); since DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa differ in architecture and pretraining, not only scale, all cross-encoder comparisons reflect both factors.
173	On DD, RoBERTa-base vanilla already achieves 0.981$\pm$0.026 (vs.\ DeBERTa 0.312$\pm$0.026), so DAPT adds only 1.3\,pp. ActorAttack exposes this as DD's simplicity: RoBERTa-base vanilla collapses to 0.466$\pm$0.163, a 51.5\,pp drop, while domain-adapted variants recover (9-class: 0.939$\pm$0.046).
174	
175	RoBERTa-large (355M) reverses the pattern on ActorAttack: vanilla F1\,=\,1.000 vs.\ 9-class 0.946, suggesting DAPT may be unnecessary or mildly harmful for strong pretrained encoders. Smaller models require DAPT to bridge 47--71\,pp. All continued pretraining reduces ActorAttack performance for the largest encoder via representation drift (Table~\ref{tab:roberta_large_lr}), though classification DAPT partially compensates on DD (+6\,pp).
176	
177	Three encoder scales reveal a scale-dependent \emph{difficulty threshold}: DeBERTa-v3-base requires adaptation for all attacks, RoBERTa-base only for complex ones, RoBERTa-large for none. A BERT-base-uncased proxy (110M, matching DeepContext) confirms cross-attack generalization is driven by DAPT, not architecture (Appendix~\ref{app:bert_proxy}). CKA analysis~\citep{kornblith2019cka} confirms DAPT reshapes representations substantially (Figure~\ref{fig:cka_heatmap}; Appendix~\ref{sec:cka_heatmap}).
178	
179	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
180	\subsection{Mixed-LLM Training}
181	
182	All preceding experiments use single-source (Qwen3-8B) data. As an exploratory pilot ($n$\,=\,10 per LLM family), we train on 250 Qwen + 250 Llama-3.1-8B conversations (Table~\ref{tab:mixed_llm}).
183	In this exploratory cross-LLM condition, 9-class achieves DD OOD F1\,=\,0.818$\pm$0.096 (+53\,pp over vanilla), while MLM drops to vanilla level (0.289). AA F1\,=\,0.504$\pm$0.107, below the majority-class baseline (F1\,$\approx$\,0.808 at 80\,:\,38 class ratio)---we attribute this to cross-LLM style confounds (Section~\ref{sec:discussion}); benign-only adaptation on Llama-origin data collapses Llama jailbreak detection (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}). Full results in Appendix~\ref{app:mixed_llm}.
184	
185	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
186	\subsection{Human Data Validation}
187	\label{sec:human_data}
188	
189	Since both training and OOD evaluation data are Qwen3-8B-generated, we validate on ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreak attempts + 204 benign conversations from real LMSYS interactions).
190	
191	\begin{table}[t]
192	\centering
193	\small
194	\caption{Human data validation on ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (macro F1, 3-seed mean$\pm$std). Bold indicates best. Single-turn data from real LMSYS user interactions, independent of Qwen3-8B.}
195	\label{tab:human_data}
196	\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}
197	\toprule
198	\textbf{Variant} & Macro F1 \\
199	\midrule
200	JB-MLM & \textbf{.837$\pm$.013} \\
201	Wiki-MLM & .791$\pm$.093 \\
202	Binary & .705$\pm$.072 \\
203	Scrambled & .616$\pm$.056 \\
204	9-class & .596$\pm$.087 \\
205	Topic & .496$\pm$.042 \\
206	\midrule
207	Vanilla & .413$\pm$.021 \\
208	\bottomrule
209	\end{tabular}
210	\end{table}
211	
212	All DAPT variants exceed vanilla by 18--42\,pp (Table~\ref{tab:human_data}), confirming transfer to human-written attacks. However, the evidence hierarchy reverses: JB-MLM leads (0.837$\pm$0.013) while 9-class ranks lower (0.596$\pm$0.087; $p < 0.001$). This reversal---analyzed in Section~\ref{sec:discussion}---reveals that classification DAPT's advantage is condition-specific to multi-turn persuasion attacks.
213	
214	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
215	\subsection{Perturbation Analysis}
216	\label{sec:perturbation}
217	
218	Under adversarial paraphrasing (BERTScore 0.92), classification-supervised variants achieve zero degradation on IID and OOD DD (Table~\ref{tab:ood_perturbation}) while non-adapted models degrade by $\sim$12\,pp ($p < 0.001$); on ActorAttack, DAPT variants degrade ($\Delta$\,=\,$-$20.4\,pp) but maintain 52\,pp over vanilla (Appendix~\ref{sec:perturbation_full}).
219	
220	Strategy trajectory analysis ($\kappa$=0.78 on known attacks, $\kappa$=0.126 on OOD; Appendix~\ref{sec:strategy_full}) confirms the taxonomy's value as ablation infrastructure rather than a detection mechanism.
221	
```

DAPT provides $\\sim$71\\,pp over both vanilla (0.258) and TF-IDF (0.261). Full per-prefix results and per-prefix error analysis in Appendix~\\ref{sec:actorattack_full} and~\\ref{app:aa_error}.\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal modeling.}\nMean pooling matches BiGRU on both DD and ActorAttack (F1 gap $\\leq$ 1\\,pp, overlapping CIs; Appendix~\\ref{app:temporal}).\nTurn-order shuffling produces negligible change ($|\\Delta| < 1$\\,pp) across both DeBERTa-v3-base 9-class (DD $\\Delta$\\,=\\,0.000, AA $\\Delta$\\,=\\,$-$0.009, IID $\\Delta$\\,=\\,0.000) and RoBERTa-large 9-class (DD $\\Delta$\\,=\\,+0.001, AA $\\Delta$\\,=\\,$-$0.007, IID $\\Delta$\\,=\\,0.000), confirming that detection relies on per-turn representations rather than sequential patterns.\n\n\\paragraph{Summary.}\nOn synthetic multi-turn data, domain exposure is the primary mechanism; domain-relevant classification objectives suffice for template attacks ($d < 0.1$ on DD/FITD) with a difficulty gradient on ActorAttack ($d$\\,=\\,0.44); label granularity, temporal modeling, and domain specificity are non-factors. Classification supervision shows a directional variance pattern ($n$\\,=\\,3--5, descriptive). User-turn distributions overlap substantially between benign and jailbreak conversations (IID 8.7$\\pm$2.1 vs.\\ 7.9$\\pm$1.5; DD 8.1$\\pm$1.5 vs.\\ 7.9$\\pm$1.5; FITD 7.8$\\pm$1.4 vs.\\ 7.9$\\pm$1.5; only AA partially separated: 5.1$\\pm$1.7 vs.\\ 7.9$\\pm$1.5); a turn-count-only baseline achieves F1\\,=\\,0.42--0.56 on IID/DD/FITD and 0.00 on AA/ToxicChat, confirming content-driven detection (Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}). Detection near-saturates at 50 conversations (Appendix~\\ref{app:data_efficiency}) and is insensitive to auxiliary loss weight (Appendix~\\ref{app:lambda}).\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Cross-Architecture Observation}\n\\label{sec:cross_encoder}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-architecture comparison on OOD Full (3-seed mean$\\pm$std). DD = Deceptive Delight, AA = ActorAttack. Bold indicates best per encoder group.}\n\\label{tab:cross_encoder}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & DD & AA \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{DeBERTa-v3-base (184M)}} \\\\[2pt]\nVanilla & .312$\\pm$.026 & .258$\\pm$.000 \\\\\nMLM & .914$\\pm$.115 & .893$\\pm$.080 \\\\\n9-class & \\textbf{.994$\\pm$.005} & \\textbf{.972$\\pm$.026} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{RoBERTa-base (125M)}} \\\\[2pt]\nVanilla & .981$\\pm$.026 & .466$\\pm$.163 \\\\\nMLM & .987$\\pm$.018 & .730$\\pm$.201 \\\\\n9-class & \\textbf{.994$\\pm$.005} & \\textbf{.939$\\pm$.046} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{RoBERTa-large (355M)}} \\\\[2pt]\nVanilla & .927$\\pm$.036 & \\textbf{1.000$\\pm$.000} \\\\\nMLM & .884$\\pm$.065 & .874$\\pm$.014 \\\\\n9-class & \\textbf{.987$\\pm$.004} & .946$\\pm$.057 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nWe replicate vanilla, MLM, and 9-class on RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_encoder}); since DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa differ in architecture and pretraining, not only scale, all cross-encoder comparisons reflect both factors.\nOn DD, RoBERTa-base vanilla already achieves 0.981$\\pm$0.026 (vs.\\ DeBERTa 0.312$\\pm$0.026), so DAPT adds only 1.3\\,pp. ActorAttack exposes this as DD's simplicity: RoBERTa-base vanilla collapses to 0.466$\\pm$0.163, a 51.5\\,pp drop, while domain-adapted variants recover (9-class: 0.939$\\pm$0.046).\n\nRoBERTa-large (355M) reverses the pattern on ActorAttack: vanilla F1\\,=\\,1.000 vs.\\ 9-class 0.946, suggesting DAPT may be unnecessary or mildly harmful for strong pretrained encoders. Smaller models require DAPT to bridge 47--71\\,pp. All continued pretraining reduces ActorAttack performance for the largest encoder via representation drift (Table~\\ref{tab:roberta_large_lr}), though classification DAPT partially compensates on DD (+6\\,pp).\n\nThree encoder scales reveal a scale-dependent \\emph{difficulty threshold}: DeBERTa-v3-base requires adaptation for all attacks, RoBERTa-base only for complex ones, RoBERTa-large for none. A BERT-base-uncased proxy (110M, matching DeepContext) confirms cross-attack generalization is driven by DAPT, not architecture (Appendix~\\ref{app:bert_proxy}). CKA analysis~\\citep{kornblith2019cka} confirms DAPT reshapes representations substantially (Figure~\\ref{fig:cka_heatmap}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:cka_heatmap}).\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Mixed-LLM Training}\n\nAll preceding experiments use single-source (Qwen3-8B) data. As an exploratory pilot ($n$\\,=\\,10 per LLM family), we train on 250 Qwen + 250 Llama-3.1-8B conversations (Table~\\ref{tab:mixed_llm}).\nIn this exploratory cross-LLM condition, 9-class achieves DD OOD F1\\,=\\,0.818$\\pm$0.096 (+53\\,pp over vanilla), while MLM drops to vanilla level (0.289). AA F1\\,=\\,0.504$\\pm$0.107, below the majority-class baseline (F1\\,$\\approx$\\,0.808 at 80\\,:\\,38 class ratio)---we attribute this to cross-LLM style confounds (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}); benign-only adaptation on Llama-origin data collapses Llama jailbreak detection (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}). Full results in Appendix~\\ref{app:mixed_llm}.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Human Data Validation}\n\\label{sec:human_data}\n\nSince both training and OOD evaluation data are Qwen3-8B-generated, we validate on ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreak attempts + 204 benign conversations from real LMSYS interactions).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Human data validation on ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (macro F1, 3-seed mean$\\pm$std). Bold indicates best. Single-turn data from real LMSYS user interactions, independent of Qwen3-8B.}\n\\label{tab:human_data}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & Macro F1 \\\\\n\\midrule\nJB-MLM & \\textbf{.837$\\pm$.013} \\\\\nWiki-MLM & .791$\\pm$.093 \\\\\nBinary & .705$\\pm$.072 \\\\\nScrambled & .616$\\pm$.056 \\\\\n9-class & .596$\\pm$.087 \\\\\nTopic & .496$\\pm$.042 \\\\\n\\midrule\nVanilla & .413$\\pm$.021 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nAll DAPT variants exceed vanilla by 18--42\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}), confirming transfer to human-written attacks. However, the evidence hierarchy reverses: JB-MLM leads (0.837$\\pm$0.013) while 9-class ranks lower (0.596$\\pm$0.087; $p < 0.001$). This reversal---analyzed in Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}---reveals that classification DAPT's advantage is condition-specific to multi-turn persuasion attacks.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Perturbation Analysis}\n\\label{sec:perturbation}\n\nUnder adversarial paraphrasing (BERTScore 0.92), classification-supervised variants achieve zero degradation on IID and OOD DD (Table~\\ref{tab:ood_perturbation}) while non-adapted models degrade by $\\sim$12\\,pp ($p < 0.001$); on ActorAttack, DAPT variants degrade ($\\Delta$\\,=\\,$-$20.4\\,pp) but maintain 52\\,pp over vanilla (Appendix~\\ref{sec:perturbation_full}).\n\nStrategy trajectory analysis ($\\kappa$=0.78 on known attacks, $\\kappa$=0.126 on OOD; Appendix~\\ref{sec:strategy_full}) confirms the taxonomy's value as ablation infrastructure rather than a detection mechanism.\n",

```text
1	% conclusion.tex — D073 Phase 2 revision (page-compressed)
2	
3	\section{Discussion}
4	\label{sec:discussion}
5	
6	\paragraph{Confounds and transferability.}
7	Three observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\,=\,0.541$\pm$0.207), scrambled labels within 3.9\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\,$\geq$\,0.97). ToxicChat (+18--42\,pp; Table~\ref{tab:human_data}) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer; mixed-LLM 9-class maintains DD F1\,=\,0.818$\pm$0.096 while MLM collapses (Appendix~\ref{app:mixed_llm}).
8	
9	\paragraph{Evidence consistent with discriminative compression.}
10	Classification DAPT is associated with feature compression into few dimensions (participation ratio 3.7--9.4 vs.\ 25--32; Fisher 2.6--19.7 vs.\ $<$0.001; Appendix~\ref{app:representation}). Scrambled labels yield comparable compression (Fisher\,=\,2.6), consistent with \citet{zhang2021understanding}, with two findings: (i)~domain-relevant partitioning is necessary (topic DAPT has higher per-turn Fisher yet fails at conversation level; Appendix~\ref{app:topic_failure}), and (ii)~compression localizes to 3 SVD dimensions in layers 8--12 (Appendix~\ref{app:layerwise}). A discriminative probe over these 3 dimensions achieves perfect AA OOD detection (sufficiency) and their removal degrades DD by 17\,pp (necessity; Appendix~\ref{app:discriminative_probe}); frozen probing near-parity (3.1\,pp) is consistent with this interpretation, though pattern memorization is not ruled out.
11	
12	\paragraph{Condition-specific advantage and boundaries.}
13	The hierarchy is specific to synthetic multi-turn evaluation. On ToxicChat (single-turn human data), JB-MLM leads (0.837) while 9-class ranks lower (0.596); 65\% of the gap stems from false positives (FPR 0.510 vs.\ 0.167), confirmed as high-confidence feature misalignment (Appendix~\ref{app:confidence}); truncation \emph{widens} the 9-class advantage (+6.0\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:truncated}). DAPT utility is scale-dependent: at $\geq$355M parameters, vanilla suffices. Mean pooling matches BiGRU ($\leq$1\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:temporal}). Benign-side shift (WildChat FPR 48.1\%$\rightarrow$68.1\%) is mitigated by 10 conversations (FPR$\rightarrow$0\%; OASST2 1.0\%$\pm$0.5\%, $N$\,=\,25; Appendix~\ref{app:oasst2}).
14	
15	\section{Conclusion}
16	
17	On synthetic multi-turn data from a single LLM, thirteen encoder configurations decompose jailbreak generalization into attack-side (discriminative compression; MLM collapses under mixed-LLM) and benign-side (FPR 58--68\%$\rightarrow$0--1.5\%, $N$\,=\,10--25) axes at 24.3$\times$ speedup; at base model scale, domain-relevant classification suffices for template attacks ($d\!<\!0.1$; AA $d$\,=\,0.44).
18	
19	\section{Limitations}
20	\label{sec:limitations}
21	
22	All findings derive from synthetic conversations generated by a single LLM (Qwen3-8B; 350 training, 76--118 test per family, 3 seeds); the evidence hierarchy reverses on human single-turn data (Section~\ref{sec:discussion}), and no human-generated multi-turn jailbreak dataset exists. OOD F1 figures are upper bounds because benign data is IID; estimated dual-OOD F1 drops 12--14\,pp without adaptation but recovers within 0.3\,pp after few-shot adaptation (Appendix~\ref{app:dual_ood}). Benign-side adaptation (WildChat FPR 68.1\%\,$\rightarrow$\,0\% with 10 conversations; OASST2 57.5\%\,$\rightarrow$\,1.0\% at $N$\,=\,25) is validated on only two platforms with small samples ($n \leq 49$). Benign-only adaptation does not address cross-LLM distribution shift: adapting on Llama-origin conversations collapses Llama jailbreak detection (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}). Only persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope; single-turn token-level attacks~\citep{zou2023gcg} and non-text modalities are not addressed.
23	
24	\section*{Ethics Statement}
25	
26	This work is defensive: it improves detection of multi-turn jailbreak attacks rather than developing new attacks. We acknowledge dual-use risk, as the persuasion taxonomy could inform adversarial actors. We mitigate this by using only synthetic conversations (no real harmful content) and withholding attack-generation prompts. Detection code, model weights, and evaluation data will be released under CC BY-NC 4.0. No human subjects were involved.
27	
```

```text
1	% related_work.tex — D048 deep writing optimization
2	
3	\section{Related Work}
4	
5	\paragraph{Multi-turn jailbreak attacks.}
6	All multi-turn jailbreak families share a persuasion substrate~\citep{zeng2024pap,hagendorff2026reasoning,li2024mhj}: Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, FITD~\citep{fitd2024}, DD~\citep{deceptivedelight2024}, and ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} each operationalize it differently, while automated methods~\citep{chao2023pair,mehrotra2024tap,perez2022redteaming} iteratively refine prompts and many-shot attacks~\citep{anil2024manyshot} exploit long-context in-context learning. Benchmarks standardize evaluation at single-turn~\citep{mazeika2024harmbench,chao2024jailbreakbench} and multi-turn~\citep{wei2023jailbroken,jiang2024redqueen,song2026multibreak} scales. A detector must generalize across families, not merely succeed on its training distribution.
7	
8	\paragraph{Multi-turn defenses and production safety.}
9	No existing defense evaluates cross-attack transfer. Conversation-level systems (DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026}, BIID~\citep{tong2025biid}, G-Guard~\citep{gguard2025}, SafeDream~\citep{safedream2026}, JailDAM~\citep{nian2025jaildam}) and representation-level approaches~\citep{lu2025xboundary,zhang2025jbshield,xie2024gradsafe,zou2024circuitbreakers,kulkarni2026latentadversarial,kadali2026trace,hua2026rcs,shen2025antidote,li2025repejailbreak,wang2026trajguard,templeton2024saemitigators} each evaluate on a single family; the latter require white-box access and can be brittle~\citep{derya2026revisitingjbshield}. J-DAPT~\citep{marchiori2025jdapt} applies CORAL-based domain adaptation with cross-modal fusion for multimodal robotic jailbreak detection; our work focuses on text-only multi-turn persuasion attacks and decomposes which adaptation factors drive cross-attack generalization through controlled ablation. Per-turn classifiers~\citep{inan2023llamaguard,metallamaguard3_2024,zeng2024shieldgemma,han2024wildguard,anthropic2025constitutional} and single-turn defenses~\citep{robey2024smoothllm,xu2024safedecoding,jain2023baseline,alon2023perplexity,phute2023selfdefense,hsu2024improved} miss multi-turn trajectories; M2S~\citep{li2025m2s} converts multi-turn attacks to single-turn as a complementary approach. Direct experimental comparison with these systems is precluded by unavailable code (DeepContext, BIID, G-Guard) and incompatible evaluation protocols (different datasets, annotation standards, and attack families); our work is positioned as a controlled ablation study rather than a SOTA competition, and we include a DeepContext-equivalent baseline (vanilla DeBERTa+BiGRU).
10	
11	\paragraph{Computational persuasion and discourse.}
12	Persuasion and discourse frameworks~\citep{austin1962speechacts,searle1969speechacts,mann1988rst,prasad2008pdtb,stolcke2000dialogueact} and technique-level classification~\citep{cialdini2001influence,zeng2024pap,hagendorff2026reasoning,dasanmartino2019fine,dasanmartino2020semeval,wang2019persuasion} offer interpretable vocabulary for attacks, but whether such structure aids detection has not been tested. Our ablation shows the taxonomy enables characterization, but detection operates through domain-adapted embeddings regardless of label correctness.
13	
14	\paragraph{Domain-adaptive pretraining.}
15	Continued pretraining on domain text yields consistent gains~\citep{gururangan2020dontstop,lee2020biobert,beltagy2019scibert,howard2018ulmfit}, but has not been disentangled for safety-critical generalization. Intermediate-task transfer~\citep{phang2018stilts} and multi-task learning~\citep{caruana1997multitask,liu2019mtdnn} confound domain exposure with supervised signal; our MLM control confirms pretraining alone closes the OOD gap. Dataset artifacts~\citep{gururangan2018artifacts,niven2019probing} motivate TF-IDF, adversarial NLP~\citep{jin2020textfooler,li2020bertattack,nasr2025attackermovessecond,andriushchenko2024adaptive} informs perturbation, and probing~\citep{conneau2018probing,hewitt2019structural} underpins CKA analysis.
16	
```

```text
Continuing through the remaining contributions, the effect sizes and performance metrics all align with their source experiments—the small effect on DD/FITD at n=10, the moderate effect on AA, and the 17 percentage point drop in DD detection when removing key directions. For the scrambled labels claim, I'm working through the numbers: the 3.9pp gap appears to be the difference between the best binary variant (0.990 on AA) and the scrambled condition (0.951), which represents the maximum performance degradation across the tested configurations. Now I'm checking the remaining claims against the experimental data: the participation ratio values (3.7-9.4 for the model variants versus 25-32 for the baselines), the Fisher statistics showing much stronger significance in the model conditions, the perfect OOD detection achieved by the top 3 dimensions on AA, and the necessity claim where removing those dimensions causes a 17-point drop in DD performance.

I'm also verifying the downstream results—JB-MLM outperforming the 9-class approach, the false positive rate accounting for most of the performance gap, and the truncation effect that gives the 9-class model an additional 6-point advantage. _source line 579 · 2026-05-23T03:02:13.796Z_
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

````text
The Director wants a writing-quality focused review, not technical consistency (which I already did). Let me re-read each section with fresh eyes focused on readability, clarity, narrative flow, and reader experience. I already have all the sections loaded from the previous review, but let me re-read them carefully with a writing lens.

Let me go section by section:

## Abstract

```
What representation properties of encoder adaptation enable cross-attack generalization in multi-turn jailbreak detection?
Multi-turn attacks spread adversarial intent across benign-seeming turns, evading existing defenses: Llama Guard~3 detects only 1.8\% of multi-turn jailbreaks.


```

Now I'm examining the key findings section—we systematically ablate thirteen encoder configurations on synthetic multi-turn data to understand what makes models generalize across different attack types. The surprising result is that domain-relevant classification objectives, even with scrambled labels, create discriminative compression that helps generalize to template attacks at base model scale, though we see a difficulty gradient depending on attack diversity. On the synthetic data, classification-based domain adaptation dramatically improves OOD detection performance while showing partial robustness.

However, there's an important caveat: these results hold on synthetic data from a single LLM, but the pattern flips when we test on human-authored single-turn data, suggesting these mechanisms are condition-specific rather than universal. Beyond attack-side generalization, benign distribution shift emerges as a practical constraint—few-shot benign adaptation is what actually brings false-positive rates down to deployment-safe levels across different data sources. So we're decomposing the problem into two independent axes: attack-side generalization through discriminative compression and benign-side robustness through few-shot adaptation, which together achieve significant inference speedup compared to using an LLM judge.

```

Now I'm noticing some structural issues in how these findings are being presented. The fourth sentence is trying to pack too much—it's discussing domain-relevant classification objectives, scrambled labels, discriminative compression, template-attack generalization, base model scale, and difficulty gradients all in one breath. The fifth sentence is similarly overloaded, attempting to cover synthetic multi-turn data results, classification-based domain adaptation, OOD detection metrics, and mixed-LLM robustness simultaneously.

The next chunk continues this pattern even more severely—it's trying to convey that domain adaptation recovers OOD detection with specific F1 scores, that template attacks remain detectable while ActorAttack degrades, that unsupervised pretraining fails, but then crucially that all of this only holds on synthetic data from a single LLM, and the pattern reverses on human-authored single-turn data. That's at least six distinct claims compressed into one sentence with semicolons and em-dashes. I need to break this apart into clearer, more digestible statements that let each finding breathe.

The introduction section looks better structured overall—the opening question is a strong hook, and the motivation in the first paragraph flows naturally. The second paragraph sets up the gap between per-turn and multi-turn defenses clearly. But I should check where the central tension gets lost or where claims start piling up again.

The third paragraph introduces a key claim about base model scale without enough context first—readers might stumble on "what does base model scale mean?" before the DeBERTa-v3 example clarifies it. The method introduction in paragraph four is clean, but then paragraph five tries to pack too many findings into the results preview: four main findings, plus attack-side details, plus benign-side details, plus cross-model comparisons. That's the same density problem as the abstract, just slightly more forgivable because there's more surrounding context.

Looking at the contributions, the first and third are crisp and well-scoped, but the second one buries its meaning in jargon—"partition sufficiency with an observed difficulty gradient" doesn't immediately land for someone encountering it fresh. The body text clarifies what it means, but the title itself might lose readers before they get there.

The method section is refreshingly concise at 51 lines, which makes sense given that the real contribution is the decomposition framework rather than the pipeline itself. Table 1 does excellent work mapping each variant to its objective. The problem formulation and two-stage separation are both crisp.

Now looking at the experiments section, which is where things get dense.

The setup is packed too tightly—the data paragraph tries to juggle IID datasets, splits, stage allocation, OOD evaluation, paraphrasing, and ToxicChat all at once. The metrics paragraph is similarly overloaded, mixing definitions, seed counts, statistical tests, confidence intervals, standard deviation conventions, and hyperparameters. These really should be broken apart.

The cross-attack generalization section has a much better narrative flow—it walks through vanilla collapse, then the MLM bridge, classification supervision, and TF-IDF comparison in logical sequence. Table 1 is clear. But line 62 is a run-on sentence that tries to pack too much: the ActorAttack results with TF-IDF collapsing while the 9-class approach maintains performance. That needs to breathe.

The decomposition section has good structure with clear headings and one point per paragraph, and the evidence hierarchy display works well. But lines 112 and 113 are both pushing 70-80 words each and become hard to parse. The summary paragraph at line 139 is the real problem—it's trying to cover seven or more findings in one dense block, and the turn-count data in parentheses makes it even harder to follow.

The cross-architecture section flows naturally, especially how the difficulty threshold concept is introduced and the CKA reference feels organic. The mixed-LLM section is appropriately brief given its exploratory scope. Human data reads cleanly with a clear table and well-presented reversal finding. The perturbation section feels almost too compressed at just six lines—a reader might question why it's a subsection at all.

In the discussion, the confounds paragraph is dense with three points crammed into semicolon-separated sentences when they'd be clearer as separate lines or bullets. The discriminative compression paragraph has been reframed well, though the technical content is still packed tightly.

The third discussion paragraph tries to juggle too many topics—ToxicChat reversal, FP decomposition, confidence analysis, truncation, scale dependence, mean pooling, and benign-side shift all in one paragraph. That's overwhelming. The conclusion is extremely compressed into a single sentence, which is space-efficient but risks feeling abrupt. Limitations are well-structured with four clear points and natural integration of the dual-OOD discussion.

Overall, the paper has a clear central question, good section structure with informative headings, and clean table design, but the abstract's fifth sentence is nearly unreadable at over 70 characters, and several discussion paragraphs try to cover too much ground at once.
````

个点自然串联。Dual-OOD 数据整合自然。长度适当（EMNLP 要求简短覆盖 3 个点左右 → 当前 4 个点，略多但可接受）。\n\n## 全文层面\n\n### AI 写作痕迹\n- 无 \"furthermore/moreover/notably/it is worth noting\" ✓\n- 无 \"-ing\" 分词开头 ✓\n- Em-dash 使用：主文约 15 处。Abstract 3 处偏多（abstract 通常不超过 1-2 对 em-dash）。Experiments 中大部分 `---` 是注释分隔符。整体在可接受范围内但偏 AI 风格。\n- 无过度分段（每段 5-8 句） ✓\n\n### 超长句问题（全文最突出的写作弱点）\n以下句子超过 60 词且含多层嵌套，建议逐一拆分：\n1. abstract.tex:7 (~70 词) — 最严重\n2. introduction.tex:26 Contribution 2 body (~80 词)\n3. experiments.tex:112 TOST 结果段 (~80 词)\n4. experiments.tex:139 Summary 段主句 (~100 词)\n\n### 信息密度评估\n| Section | 密度 | 评估 |\n|---------|------|------|\n| Abstract | 过高 | 第 5 句需拆分 |\n| Introduction | 偏高但可接受 | 第 5 段可压缩 |\n| Method | 恰当 | ✓ |\n| Experiments | 偏高 | Metrics 段和 Summary 段需整理 |\n| Discussion | 偏高 | 第 3 段需拆分 |\n| Conclusion | 极简 | 可加半句 |\n| Limitations | 恰当 | ✓ |\n\n### 读者体验总评\n核心 contribution（两轴分解 + scrambled 等价 + discriminative subspace）在 Introduction 中表达清楚。一个 EMNLP 审稿人第一遍读 **能理解核心贡献**，但 Abstract 第 5 句和 Experiments Summary 段会造成认知负担。改善这 4-5 个超长句后，整体可读性会显著提升。\n\n全文无逻辑断层，story arc 连贯。",

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   I am the Research Reviewer (Loop #7, continued) for the `persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect` project, independently auditing experiment conclusions, worker outputs, and paper writing quality. This session covered:
   - Reviewing rebuttal_evidence_matrix.md worker output (data accuracy verification)

   - Full-paper writing quality review (readability, sentence length, information density, AI style)

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **DisPeD**: Two-stage pipeline — Stage 1 (encoder adaptation, 7 DeBERTa variants) + Stage 2 (frozen BiGRU classification)
   - **Two-axis generalization framework**: attack-side (DAPT/discriminative compression) and benign-side (few-shot adaptation)
   - **Evidence hierarchy**: vanilla < topic < wiki-MLM ≈ jb-MLM < scrambled = 9-class ≈ binary (on synthetic OOD)
   - **ToxicChat reversal**: On human single-turn data, hierarchy reverses — JB-MLM (0.837) > 9-class (0.596)
   - **Discriminative compression**: Classification DAPT compresses to 3 SVD dimensions (89.8% variance) in layers 8-12
   - **SVD sufficiency + partial necessity**: exp54 probe F1=1.0 with 3 dims (sufficiency); exp65 ablation DD drops 16.9pp when removing top-3 (partial necessity, FITD unaffected)
   - **Dual-OOD F1**: Without benign adaptation, F1 drops 12-14pp; after 10-25 shot adaptation, recovers within 0.3pp of IID
   - **TOST equivalence**: Template attacks d<0.1 (DD/FITD), complexity-dependent boundary d=0.44 (ActorAttack)
   - **Confidence analysis (exp64)**: 9-class FPs are 94-98% high-confidence per seed (>0.9, median=1.0), JB-MLM FPs 41-69% high-confidence
   - **EMNLP 2026 ARR**: 8-page main text limit, DDL 2026-05-25, Overall Assessment scale 1-5 (3.0 = Findings)
   - **13 encoder configurations**: 7 DeBERTa-v3 + 3 RoBERTa-base + 3 RoBERTa-large

3. Files and Code Sections:
   - **`docs/paper/abstract.tex`** (D081 updated)
     - Line 6: "are associated with" (D081 causal downgrade from "trigger") + "at base model scale" (SF9) + "observed difficulty gradient on attacks employing more varied strategies" (MF2)

```json
        "content": "% related_work.tex — D048 deep writing optimization\n\n\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-turn jailbreak attacks.}\nAll multi-turn jailbreak families share a persuasion substrate~\\citep{zeng2024pap,hagendorff2026reasoning,li2024mhj}: Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, FITD~\\citep{fitd2024}, DD~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024}, and ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} each operationalize it differently, while automated methods~\\citep{chao2023pair,mehrotra2024tap,perez2022redteaming} iteratively refine prompts and many-shot attacks~\\citep{anil2024manyshot} exploit long-context in-context learning. Benchmarks standardize evaluation at single-turn~\\citep{mazeika2024harmbench,chao2024jailbreakbench} and multi-turn~\\citep{wei2023jailbroken,jiang2024redqueen,song2026multibreak} scales. A detector must generalize across families, not merely succeed on its training distribution.\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-turn defenses and production safety.}\nNo existing defense evaluates cross-attack transfer. Conversation-level systems (DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026}, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid}, G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025}, SafeDream~\\citep{safedream2026}, JailDAM~\\citep{nian2025jaildam}) and representation-level approaches~\\citep{lu2025xboundary,zhang2025jbshield,xie2024gradsafe,zou2024circuitbreakers,kulkarni2026latentadversarial,kadali2026trace,hua2026rcs,shen2025antidote,li2025repejailbreak,wang2026trajguard,templeton2024saemitigators} each evaluate on a single family; the latter require white-box access and can be brittle~\\citep{derya2026revisitingjbshield}. J-DAPT~\\citep{marchiori2025jdapt} applies CORAL-based domain adaptation with cross-modal fusion for multimodal robotic jailbreak detection; our work focuses on text-only multi-turn persuasion attacks and decomposes which adaptation factors drive cross-attack generalization through controlled ablation. Per-turn classifiers~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard,metallamaguard3_2024,zeng2024shieldgemma,han2024wildguard,anthropic2025constitutional} and single-turn defenses~\\citep{robey2024smoothllm,xu2024safedecoding,jain2023baseline,alon2023perplexity,phute2023selfdefense,hsu2024improved} miss multi-turn trajectories; M2S~\\citep{li2025m2s} converts multi-turn attacks to single-turn as a complementary approach. Direct experimental comparison with these systems is precluded by unavailable code (DeepContext, BIID, G-Guard) and incompatible evaluation protocols (different datasets, annotation standards, and attack families); our work is positioned as a controlled ablation study rather than a SOTA competition, and we include a DeepContext-equivalent baseline (vanilla DeBERTa+BiGRU).\n\n\\paragraph{Computational persuasion and discourse.}\nPersuasion and discourse frameworks~\\citep{austin1962speechacts,searle1969speechacts,mann1988rst,prasad2008pdtb,stolcke2000dialogueact} and technique-level classification~\\citep{cialdini2001influence,zeng2024pap,hagendorff2026reasoning,dasanmartino2019fine,dasanmartino2020semeval,wang2019persuasion} offer interpretable vocabulary for attacks, but whether such structure aids detection has not been tested. Our ablation shows the taxonomy enables characterization, but detection operates through domain-adapted embeddings regardless of label correctness.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-adaptive pretraining.}\nContinued pretraining on domain text yields consistent gains~\\citep{gururangan2020dontstop,lee2020biobert,beltagy2019scibert,howard2018ulmfit}, but has not been disentangled for safety-critical generalization. Intermediate-task transfer~\\citep{phang2018stilts} and multi-task learning~\\citep{caruana1997multitask,liu2019mtdnn} confound domain exposure with supervised signal; our MLM control confirms pretraining alone closes the OOD gap. Dataset artifacts~\\citep{gururangan2018artifacts,niven2019probing} motivate TF-IDF, adversarial NLP~\\citep{jin2020textfooler,li2020bertattack,nasr2025attackermovessecond,andriushchenko2024adaptive} informs perturbation, and probing~\\citep{conneau2018probing,hewitt2019structural} underpins CKA analysis.\n",
        "numLines": 16,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 16
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/related_work.tex"
  },
```

```json
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/conclusion.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/conclusion.tex",
        "content": "% conclusion.tex — D073 Phase 2 revision (page-compressed)\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Confounds and transferability.}\nThree observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207), scrambled labels within 3.9\\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97). ToxicChat (+18--42\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer; mixed-LLM 9-class maintains DD F1\\,=\\,0.818$\\pm$0.096 while MLM collapses (Appendix~\\ref{app:mixed_llm}).\n\n\\paragraph{Evidence consistent with discriminative compression.}\nClassification DAPT is associated with feature compression into few dimensions (participation ratio 3.7--9.4 vs.\\ 25--32; Fisher 2.6--19.7 vs.\\ $<$0.001; Appendix~\\ref{app:representation}). Scrambled labels yield comparable compression (Fisher\\,=\\,2.6), consistent with \\citet{zhang2021understanding}, with two findings: (i)~domain-relevant partitioning is necessary (topic DAPT has higher per-turn Fisher yet fails at conversation level; Appendix~\\ref{app:topic_failure}), and (ii)~compression localizes to 3 SVD dimensions in layers 8--12 (Appendix~\\ref{app:layerwise}). A discriminative probe over these 3 dimensions achieves perfect AA OOD detection (sufficiency) and their removal degrades DD by 17\\,pp while FITD is unaffected (partial necessity; Appendix~\\ref{app:discriminative_probe}); frozen probing near-parity (3.1\\,pp) is consistent with this interpretation.\n\n\\paragraph{Condition-specific advantage and boundaries.}\nThe hierarchy is specific to synthetic multi-turn evaluation. On ToxicChat, JB-MLM leads (0.837) while 9-class ranks lower (0.596); 65\\% of the gap stems from false positives (FPR 0.510 vs.\\ 0.167), confirmed as high-confidence misalignment (Appendix~\\ref{app:confidence}); truncation widens the 9-class advantage (+6.0\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:truncated}). DAPT utility is scale-dependent: at $\\geq$355M, vanilla suffices; mean pooling matches BiGRU ($\\leq$1\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:temporal}). Benign-side shift (WildChat FPR 48.1\\% (vanilla) to 68.1\\% (9-class)) is mitigated by 10 conversations (FPR$\\rightarrow$0\\%; OASST2 1.0\\%$\\pm$0.5\\%, $N$\\,=\\,25; Appendix~\\ref{app:oasst2}).\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\nOn synthetic multi-turn data, thirteen encoders decompose jailbreak generalization into attack-side (discriminative compression; MLM collapses under mixed-LLM) and benign-side (FPR 58--68\\%$\\rightarrow$0--1.5\\%, $N$\\,$\\leq$\\,25) axes at 24.3$\\times$ speedup; classification suffices for template attacks ($d\\!<\\!0.1$; AA $d$\\,=\\,0.44) at base scale; the hierarchy reverses on human data, confirming condition specificity.\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nAll findings derive from synthetic conversations generated by a single LLM (Qwen3-8B; 350 training, 76--118 test per family, 3 seeds); the evidence hierarchy reverses on human single-turn data (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}), and no human-generated multi-turn jailbreak dataset exists. OOD F1 figures are upper bounds because benign data is IID; estimated dual-OOD F1 drops 12--14\\,pp without adaptation but recovers within 0.3\\,pp after few-shot adaptation (Appendix~\\ref{app:dual_ood}). Benign-side adaptation (WildChat FPR 68.1\\%\\,$\\rightarrow$\\,0\\% with 10 conversations; OASST2 57.5\\%\\,$\\rightarrow$\\,1.0\\% at $N$\\,=\\,25) is validated on only two platforms with small samples ($n \\leq 49$). Benign-only adaptation does not address cross-LLM distribution shift: adapting on Llama-origin conversations collapses Llama jailbreak detection (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}). Only persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope; single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg} and non-text modalities are not addressed.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work is defensive: it improves detection of multi-turn jailbreak attacks rather than developing new attacks. We acknowledge dual-use risk, as the persuasion taxonomy could inform adversarial actors. We mitigate this by using only synthetic conversations (no real harmful content) and withholding attack-generation prompts. Detection code, model weights, and evaluation data will be released under CC BY-NC 4.0. No human subjects were involved.\n",
        "numLines": 27,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 27
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/conclusion.tex"
  },
```

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "097a1a93-2084-46ff-b981-4c9914c4ba34",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
        "content": "% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door~\\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \\citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.\n\nExisting defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and miss multi-turn manipulation; Llama Guard~3 detects only 1.8\\% of multi-turn jailbreaks in our evaluation (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}). Multi-turn defenses (DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026}, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid}, G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025}) model conversation-level structure but each evaluates on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \\emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?}\n\nPretrained language understanding does not suffice at base model scale: vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\\ DeepContext) drops from F1\\,=\\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on OOD Deceptive Delight. LLM-as-judge baselines also struggle (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}), though prompt optimization may improve their performance. The bottleneck is distributional: cross-attack generalization requires domain adaptation, not a larger model.\n\nOur pipeline, \\textbf{DisPeD}\\footnote{DisPeD decomposes the detection pipeline into independently controllable stages---encoder adaptation (Stage~1) and temporal aggregation (Stage~2)---to isolate each factor's contribution to cross-attack generalization.} (Disentangled Pattern Detection), separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU; Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture}). Seven Stage~1 variants systematically vary training objectives while sharing the same two-stage pipeline (Section~\\ref{sec:method}); the contribution is the decomposition, not the pipeline.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_architecture.pdf}\n\\caption{DisPeD architecture. Stage~1 adapts a DeBERTa encoder on jailbreak conversation text (seven variant objectives). Stage~2 aggregates frozen \\texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via BiGRU for conversation-level jailbreak detection. Only user turns are processed.}\n\\label{fig:architecture}\n\\end{figure}\n\nControlled ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families and two training compositions yields four findings: scrambled-label equivalence on template attacks, MLM fragility under mixed-LLM conditions, discriminative subspace concentration (3 of 768 dimensions suffice), and a two-axis decomposition of generalization. On the \\emph{attack side}, vanilla DeBERTa drops to F1\\,=\\,0.26--0.31 on OOD attacks; classification DAPT recovers to 0.97--1.0, while MLM collapses under mixed-LLM training (F1\\,=\\,0.289 $\\approx$ vanilla). On the \\emph{benign side}, distributional mismatch drives false positives: WildChat FPR reaches 68.1\\%, but 10--25 target-domain conversations reduce it to 0--1.5\\% across two sources. RoBERTa-large (355M) handles ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation. Classification supervision shows a directional variance pattern on synthetic data ($n$\\,=\\,3--5, descriptive) that does not hold on human data (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}).\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]\n  \\item \\textbf{Systematic ablation of cross-attack generalization factors.} On synthetic multi-turn data, thirteen encoder configurations at three scales (125--355M) decompose jailbreak detection into two orthogonal axes---attack-side and benign-side---the first such controlled factorial study in this domain. On the attack side, classification DAPT recovers OOD detection (vanilla 0.26--0.31 $\\rightarrow$ 0.97--1.0) while unsupervised pretraining collapses under mixed-LLM training. On the benign side, 10--25 target-domain conversations reduce FPR from 58--68\\% to 0--1.5\\% on two independent sources (WildChat, OASST2; IID evaluation) without detection degradation.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Partition sufficiency with an observed difficulty gradient (at base model scale).} Domain-relevant classification objectives---including scrambled labels---suffice for cross-attack generalization on template attacks (TOST-confirmed, $d < 0.1$ on DD/FITD at $n$\\,=\\,10), while attacks employing more varied conversational strategies (ActorAttack) show a moderate label effect ($d$\\,=\\,0.44). This is consistent with \\citet{zhang2021understanding}'s framework, with two domain-specific findings: domain-relevant partitioning is necessary (topic labels insufficient) and compression localizes to 3 SVD dimensions in layers 8--12; a discriminative probe confirms sufficiency, and removing these 3 directions degrades DD detection by 17\\,pp while FITD is unaffected (partial necessity; Appendix~\\ref{app:discriminative_probe}).\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Two-step adaptation protocol.} On the synthetic benchmark, continued pretraining achieves 24.3$\\times$ inference speedup over LLM-as-judge (a deployment cost comparison; parameter counts differ by 43$\\times$; Appendix~\\ref{app:efficiency}); 10--25 target-domain benign conversations reduce FPR below deployment thresholds on both WildChat and OASST2 crowdsourced conversations. No target-domain attack data is required.\n\\end{itemize}\n",
        "numLines": 30,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 30
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/introduction.tex"
  },
```

```json
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/abstract.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/abstract.tex",
        "content": "% abstract.tex — D073 Phase 2 revision\n\\begin{abstract}\nWhat representation properties of encoder adaptation enable cross-attack generalization in multi-turn jailbreak detection?\nMulti-turn attacks spread adversarial intent across benign-seeming turns, evading existing defenses: Llama Guard~3 detects only 1.8\\% of multi-turn jailbreaks.\nWe decompose cross-attack generalization through controlled ablation of thirteen encoder configurations on synthetic multi-turn data generated by a single LLM (Qwen3-8B).\nA surprising finding emerges: domain-relevant classification objectives---including scrambled labels---are associated with discriminative compression sufficient for template-attack generalization at base model scale, with an observed difficulty gradient on attacks employing more varied strategies.\nOn synthetic multi-turn data, classification-based domain adaptation recovers OOD detection (F1\\,=\\,0.97--1.0 vs.\\ vanilla 0.26--0.31; attack-side OOD, benign IID) and shows partial robustness under mixed-LLM training---template attacks remain detectable while strategy-diverse ActorAttack degrades (F1\\,=\\,0.504)---while unsupervised pretraining collapses; however, these findings are established on synthetic data from a single LLM, and the evidence hierarchy reverses on human-authored single-turn data indicating condition-specific rather than universal mechanisms.\nOrthogonal to attack-side generalization, benign distribution shift is a practical bottleneck: few-shot benign adaptation reduces false-positive rates below deployment thresholds on two independent sources.\nThese findings decompose multi-turn jailbreak generalization into two orthogonal axes---attack-side (discriminative compression) and benign-side (few-shot adaptation)---with 24.3$\\times$ inference speedup over LLM-as-judge.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
        "numLines": 11,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 11
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/abstract.tex"
  },
```

```text
3	\section{Introduction}
4	
5	Multi-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door~\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.
6	
7	Existing defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and miss multi-turn manipulation; Llama Guard~3 detects only 1.8\% of multi-turn jailbreaks in our evaluation (Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}). Multi-turn defenses (DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026}, BIID~\citep{tong2025biid}, G-Guard~\citep{gguard2025}) model conversation-level structure but each evaluates on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?}
8	
9	Pretrained language understanding does not suffice at base model scale: vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\ DeepContext) drops from F1\,=\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on OOD Deceptive Delight. LLM-as-judge baselines also struggle (Appendix~\ref{sec:llm_judge}), though prompt optimization may improve their performance. The bottleneck is distributional: cross-attack generalization requires domain adaptation, not a larger model.
10	
11	Our pipeline, \textbf{DisPeD}\footnote{DisPeD decomposes the detection pipeline into independently controllable stages---encoder adaptation (Stage~1) and temporal aggregation (Stage~2)---to isolate each factor's contribution to cross-attack generalization.} (Disentangled Pattern Detection), separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU; Figure~\ref{fig:architecture}). Seven Stage~1 variants systematically vary training objectives while sharing the same two-stage pipeline (Section~\ref{sec:method}); the contribution is the decomposition, not the pipeline.
12	
13	\begin{figure}[t]
14	\centering
15	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_architecture.pdf}
16	\caption{DisPeD architecture. Stage~1 adapts a DeBERTa encoder on jailbreak conversation text (seven variant objectives). Stage~2 aggregates frozen \texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via BiGRU for conversation-level jailbreak detection. Only user turns are processed.}
17	\label{fig:architecture}
18	\end{figure}
19	
20	Controlled ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families and two training compositions yields four findings: scrambled-label equivalence on template attacks, MLM fragility under mixed-LLM conditions, discriminative subspace concentration (3 of 768 dimensions suffice), and a two-axis decomposition of generalization. On the \emph{attack side}, vanilla DeBERTa drops to F1\,=\,0.26--0.31 on OOD attacks; classification DAPT recovers to 0.97--1.0, while MLM collapses under mixed-LLM training (F1\,=\,0.289 $\approx$ vanilla). On the \emph{benign side}, distributional mismatch drives false positives: WildChat FPR reaches 68.1\%, but 10--25 target-domain conversations reduce it to 0--1.5\% across two sources. RoBERTa-large (355M) handles ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation. Classification supervision shows a directional variance pattern on synthetic data ($n$\,=\,3--5, descriptive) that does not hold on human data (Section~\ref{sec:human_data}).
21	
22	Our contributions:
23	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]
24	  \item \textbf{Systematic ablation of cross-attack generalization factors.} On synthetic multi-turn data, thirteen encoder configurations at three scales (125--355M) decompose jailbreak detection into two orthogonal axes---attack-side and benign-side---the first such controlled factorial study in this domain. On the attack side, classification DAPT recovers OOD detection (vanilla 0.26--0.31 $\rightarrow$ 0.97--1.0) while unsupervised pretraining collapses under mixed-LLM training. On the benign side, 10--25 target-domain conversations reduce FPR from 58--68\% to 0--1.5\% on two independent sources (WildChat, OASST2; IID evaluation) without detection degradation.
25	
26	  \item \textbf{Partition sufficiency with an observed difficulty gradient (at base model scale).} Domain-relevant classification objectives---including scrambled labels---suffice for cross-attack generalization on template attacks (TOST-confirmed, $d < 0.1$ on DD/FITD at $n$\,=\,10), while attacks employing more varied conversational strategies (ActorAttack) show a moderate label effect ($d$\,=\,0.44). This is consistent with \citet{zhang2021understanding}'s framework, with two domain-specific findings: domain-relevant partitioning is necessary (topic labels insufficient) and compression localizes to 3 SVD dimensions in layers 8--12; a discriminative probe confirms sufficiency, and removing these 3 directions degrades DD detection by 17\,pp while FITD is unaffected (partial necessity; Appendix~\ref{app:discriminative_probe}).
27	
28	  \item \textbf{Two-step adaptation protocol.} On the synthetic benchmark, continued pretraining achieves 24.3$\times$ inference speedup over LLM-as-judge (a deployment cost comparison; parameter counts differ by 43$\times$; Appendix~\ref{app:efficiency}); 10--25 target-domain benign conversations reduce FPR below deployment thresholds on both WildChat and OASST2 crowdsourced conversations. No target-domain attack data is required.
29	\end{itemize}
30	
```

```text
1	% abstract.tex — D073 Phase 2 revision
2	\begin{abstract}
3	What representation properties of encoder adaptation enable cross-attack generalization in multi-turn jailbreak detection?
4	Multi-turn attacks spread adversarial intent across benign-seeming turns, evading existing defenses: Llama Guard~3 detects only 1.8\% of multi-turn jailbreaks.
5	We decompose cross-attack generalization through controlled ablation of thirteen encoder configurations on synthetic multi-turn data generated by a single LLM (Qwen3-8B).
6	A surprising finding emerges: domain-relevant classification objectives---including scrambled labels---are associated with discriminative compression sufficient for template-attack generalization at base model scale, with an observed difficulty gradient on attacks employing more varied strategies.
7	On synthetic multi-turn data, classification-based domain adaptation recovers OOD detection (F1\,=\,0.97--1.0 vs.\ vanilla 0.26--0.31; attack-side OOD, benign IID) and shows partial robustness under mixed-LLM training---template attacks remain detectable while strategy-diverse ActorAttack degrades (F1\,=\,0.504)---while unsupervised pretraining collapses; however, these findings are established on synthetic data from a single LLM, and the evidence hierarchy reverses on human-authored single-turn data indicating condition-specific rather than universal mechanisms.
8	Orthogonal to attack-side generalization, benign distribution shift is a practical bottleneck: few-shot benign adaptation reduces false-positive rates below deployment thresholds on two independent sources.
9	These findings decompose multi-turn jailbreak generalization into two orthogonal axes---attack-side (discriminative compression) and benign-side (few-shot adaptation)---with 24.3$\times$ inference speedup over LLM-as-judge.
10	\end{abstract}
11
```
