55	\end{table}
56	
57	Vanilla encoders collapse under cross-attack transfer; domain-adapted variants recover near-perfect detection (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}, Figure~\ref{fig:cross_attack_bar}).
58	DD exhibits high lexical separability (TF-IDF F1\,=\,0.961); ActorAttack, where TF-IDF collapses to 0.261, serves as the primary stress test for beyond-lexical generalization.
59	On OOD DD, vanilla DeBERTa collapses to F1 = 0.312$\pm$0.026, worsening with more turns (0.628 at $K$=1 to 0.312 at Full), consistent with attack turns occupying ``benign'' embedding regions~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}.
60	MLM achieves mean F1 $\geq$ 0.97 at all prefixes $K \leq 5$ without any classification labels; domain exposure alone is sufficient.
61	At full conversation length, MLM drops to 0.914$\pm$0.115 (seed 456 recall = 0.64), while the 9-class model maintains 0.994$\pm$0.005.
62	TF-IDF LR reaches F1 = 0.961 on full DD; DD is fully solvable by lexical features.
63	On strategy-diverse ActorAttack, TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 (precision = 1.0, recall = 0.15), while 9-class maintains 0.972$\pm$0.026: a 71\,pp gap. Lexical methods cannot capture multi-persona role-playing attack structure. This establishes a ``difficulty threshold'' for lexical methods parallel to that of vanilla encoders: both succeed on DD and fail on ActorAttack (Section~\ref{sec:limitations}).
64	Seed-level F1 values confirm all pairwise conclusions hold at every seed (Appendix~\ref{app:seed_level}); pooled BCa CIs provide exploratory confirmation (9-class DD Full [0.943, 1.000] vs.\ vanilla [0.202, 0.341]; all permutation $p < 0.0001$). FITD serves as a near-ceiling baseline where all variants achieve F1\,$\geq$\,0.990, providing limited discriminative power.
65	
66	\begin{figure}[t]
67	\centering
68	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig2_cross_attack_bar.pdf}
69	\caption{Cross-attack generalization (full conversation F1). DisPeD (9-class) maintains near-perfect detection across all OOD families. On ActorAttack, both vanilla DeBERTa and TF-IDF collapse ($\leq$0.26), exposing a difficulty threshold that lexical and non-adapted methods cannot cross.}
70	\label{fig:cross_attack_bar}
71	\end{figure}
72	
73	Prefix-length analysis (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:full_prefix}) shows vanilla F1 drops from 0.628 at $K$=1 to 0.312 at full length. MLM achieves strong early detection ($K$=2: 0.993) but is unstable at full length (0.914$\pm$0.115); the 9-class model maintains 0.972--0.994 across all prefixes, demonstrating that classification supervision stabilizes against distributional drift.
74	
75	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
76	\subsection{Decomposing the Detection Mechanism}
77	\label{sec:decomposition}
78	
79	\begin{table}[t]
80	\centering
81	\small
82	\caption{Evidence hierarchy on OOD DD and ActorAttack (AA) at full conversation length (3-seed mean$\pm$std where available). Bold indicates best per column. OOD F1 reflects attack-side generalization only; benign samples are IID from training distribution (benign-side shift evaluated in Section~\ref{sec:discussion}). Full per-prefix results in Appendix Tables~\ref{tab:full_prefix} and~\ref{tab:actorattack_full}.}
83	\label{tab:evidence_hierarchy}
84	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
85	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
86	\toprule
87	\textbf{Variant} & DD-Full & AA-Full & DD-$K$=2 \\
88	\midrule
89	9-class (full model) & .994$\pm$.005 & .972$\pm$.026 & .981$\pm$.013 \\
90	Binary (2-class) & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} & \textbf{.990$\pm$.008} & .994$\pm$.009 \\
91	Scrambled labels & .994$\pm$.005 & .951$\pm$.043 & .981$\pm$.026 \\
92	MLM-only$^{\ddagger}$ (no labels) & .914$\pm$.115 & .893$\pm$.080 & .993$\pm$.009 \\
93	9-class + mean pool$^{*}$ & .991$\pm$.013 & .962$\pm$.015 & \textbf{1.000} \\
94	\midrule
95	Topic classif.\ (5-class) & .541$\pm$.207 & .625$\pm$.141 & .593$\pm$.166 \\
96	Wikipedia MLM & .904$\pm$.115 & .686$\pm$.297 & .911$\pm$.073 \\
97	Vanilla DeBERTa & .312$\pm$.026 & .258$\pm$.000 & .479$\pm$.038 \\
98	TF-IDF LR$^{\dagger}$ & .961 & .261 & \textbf{1.000} \\
99	Llama Guard~3$^{\P}$ & .025 & .000 & -- \\
100	\bottomrule
101	\end{tabular}}
102	\vspace{-2pt}
103	{\footnotesize $^{*}$9-class encoder with mean pooling replacing BiGRU. $^{\dagger}$Deterministic (no std). $^{\ddagger}$MLM-only IID (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}) is single-seed (42); all OOD values are 3-seed. $^{\P}$Zero-shot; no domain training. Population std reported; Bessel-corrected values are 22\% larger at $n$=3.}
104	\end{table}
105	
106	A clear \emph{evidence hierarchy} emerges across seven variants (Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}), ordered by OOD detection at full conversation length on DD:
107	
108	\vspace{2pt}
109	\noindent\hspace{0.5em}{\small vanilla (0.312$\pm$0.026) $<$ topic (0.541$\pm$0.207) $\ll$ wiki-MLM (0.904$\pm$0.115) $\approx$ jb-MLM (0.914$\pm$0.115) $<$ scrambled (0.994$\pm$0.005) $=$ 9-class (0.994$\pm$0.005) $\approx$ binary (1.000$\pm$0.000)}
110	\vspace{2pt}
111	
112	\paragraph{Domain exposure is the primary mechanism.}
113	MLM bridges $\sim$60\,pp from vanilla (0.312$\pm$0.026 $\to$ 0.914$\pm$0.115) without labels; this supports the DAPT interpretation~\citep{gururangan2020dontstop}. TF-IDF collapse on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\ 9-class 0.972) confirms that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond lexical overlap.
114	All three classification-supervised variants converge: all tested domain-relevant partitions suffice for near-perfect detection (F1 = 0.994--1.000 on DD). Extended to $n$\,=\,10 seeds, TOST (margin = 5\,pp) confirms equivalence on DD ($p < 10^{-8}$, $d$\,=\,$-$0.14; Table~\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}) and FITD ($p < 10^{-8}$, $d \approx 0$). An observed difficulty gradient emerges: template attacks show full equivalence ($d < 0.1$), while strategy-diverse ActorAttack retains moderate label sensitivity ($d$\,=\,0.44, $p$\,=\,0.183).
115	Label granularity (binary vs.\ 9-class) is a non-factor; 10 random binary partitions confirm this (IID/DD: 10/10 perfect; AA: 9/10; Appendix~\ref{app:random_partition}).
116	Classification supervision shows a directional variance pattern on synthetic OOD (std 0.005 vs.\ 0.115 on DD, $n$\,=\,3--5; descriptive, not statistically tested). This pattern does not hold on human-authored data (Section~\ref{sec:human_data}).
117	Appendix~\ref{app:tsne} provides t-SNE visualizations confirming that domain-adapted variants produce tighter, more separable embedding clusters than the vanilla baseline.
118	
119	\paragraph{Domain specificity is marginal at full length but meaningful for early detection.}
120	Wikipedia MLM (0.904$\pm$0.115) nearly matches jailbreak-domain MLM at full length (1\,pp gap), though jailbreak-domain text provides an 8\,pp advantage at early prefixes ($K$=2: 0.993 vs.\ 0.911).
121	Perturbation analysis (Section~\ref{sec:perturbation}) reveals a sharper domain specificity effect: Wikipedia MLM degrades by $\sim$12\,pp under adversarial paraphrasing while jailbreak-domain MLM degrades by only 2.6\,pp.
122	
123	\paragraph{Misaligned supervision destabilizes generalization.}
124	Topic classification yields DD-Full F1 = 0.541$\pm$0.207; seed 123 falls \emph{below} vanilla (0.252 vs.\ 0.312). Despite using the same jailbreak-domain training data, topic classification performs worse than Wikipedia MLM (0.904$\pm$0.115), which uses out-of-domain text with no labels. A misaligned objective forces the encoder to allocate capacity to irrelevant features and actively destabilizes representations that MLM would preserve; embedding analysis confirms higher per-turn separability yet failed temporal aggregation (Appendix~\ref{app:topic_failure}).
125	
126	\paragraph{Evidence hierarchy on strategy-diverse attacks.}
127	ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} uses multi-persona steering across longer conversation structures and provides a more challenging OOD benchmark than DD.
128	The evidence hierarchy at full conversation length is:
129	
130	\vspace{2pt}
131	\noindent\hspace{0.5em}{\small TF-IDF (0.261) $\approx$ vanilla (0.258$\pm$0.000) $<$ topic (0.625$\pm$0.141) $<$ wiki-MLM (0.686$\pm$0.297) $<$ jb-MLM (0.893$\pm$0.080) $<$ scrambled (0.951$\pm$0.043) $\approx$ 9-class (0.972$\pm$0.026) $\approx$ binary (0.990$\pm$0.008)}
132	\vspace{2pt}
133	
134	TF-IDF collapses from 0.961 (DD) to 0.261 (ActorAttack, precision = 1.0, recall = 0.15). This gap shows that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond bag-of-words.
135	Classification-supervised variants cluster tightly (0.951--0.990, max gap 3.9\,pp at $n$\,=\,3), though 10-seed TOST resolves the 9-class vs.\ scrambled gap as a moderate effect ($d$\,=\,0.44). DAPT provides $\sim$71\,pp over both vanilla (0.258) and TF-IDF (0.261). Full per-prefix results and per-prefix error analysis in Appendix~\ref{sec:actorattack_full} and~\ref{app:aa_error}.
136	
137	\paragraph{Temporal modeling.}
138	Mean pooling matches BiGRU on both DD and ActorAttack (F1 gap $\leq$ 1\,pp, overlapping CIs; Appendix~\ref{app:temporal}).
139	Turn-order shuffling produces negligible change ($|\Delta| < 1$\,pp) across both DeBERTa-v3-base 9-class (DD $\Delta$\,=\,0.000, AA $\Delta$\,=\,$-$0.009, IID $\Delta$\,=\,0.000) and RoBERTa-large 9-class (DD $\Delta$\,=\,+0.001, AA $\Delta$\,=\,$-$0.007, IID $\Delta$\,=\,0.000), confirming that detection relies on per-turn representations rather than sequential patterns.
140	
141	\paragraph{Summary.}
142	On synthetic multi-turn data, domain exposure is the primary mechanism; domain-relevant classification objectives suffice for template attacks ($d < 0.1$ on DD/FITD) with a difficulty gradient on ActorAttack ($d$\,=\,0.44); label granularity, temporal modeling, and domain specificity are non-factors. Classification supervision shows a directional variance pattern ($n$\,=\,3--5, descriptive). User-turn distributions overlap substantially between benign and jailbreak conversations (IID 8.7$\pm$2.1 vs.\ 7.9$\pm$1.5; DD 8.1$\pm$1.5 vs.\ 7.9$\pm$1.5; FITD 7.8$\pm$1.4 vs.\ 7.9$\pm$1.5; only AA partially separated: 5.1$\pm$1.7 vs.\ 7.9$\pm$1.5); a turn-count-only baseline achieves F1\,=\,0.42--0.56 on IID/DD/FITD and 0.00 on AA/ToxicChat, confirming content-driven detection (Appendix~\ref{app:length_independence}). Detection near-saturates at 50 conversations (Appendix~\ref{app:data_efficiency}) and is insensitive to auxiliary loss weight (Appendix~\ref{app:lambda}).
143

A misaligned objective forces the encoder to allocate capacity to irrelevant features and actively destabilizes representations that MLM would preserve; embedding analysis confirms higher per-turn separability yet failed temporal aggregation (Appendix~\\ref{app:topic_failure}).\n\n\\paragraph{Evidence hierarchy on strategy-diverse attacks.}\nActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} uses multi-persona steering across longer conversation structures and provides a more challenging OOD benchmark than DD.\nThe evidence hierarchy at full conversation length is:\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\noindent\\hspace{0.5em}{\\small TF-IDF (0.261) $\\approx$ vanilla (0.258$\\pm$0.000) $<$ topic (0.625$\\pm$0.141) $<$ wiki-MLM (0.686$\\pm$0.297) $<$ jb-MLM (0.893$\\pm$0.080) $<$ scrambled (0.951$\\pm$0.043) $\\approx$ 9-class (0.972$\\pm$0.026) $\\approx$ binary (0.990$\\pm$0.008)}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\nTF-IDF collapses from 0.961 (DD) to 0.261 (ActorAttack, precision = 1.0, recall = 0.15). This gap shows that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond bag-of-words.\nClassification-supervised variants cluster tightly (0.951--0.990, max gap 3.9\\,pp at $n$\\,=\\,3), though 10-seed TOST resolves the 9-class vs.\\ scrambled gap as a moderate effect ($d$\\,=\\,0.44). DAPT provides $\\sim$71\\,pp over both vanilla (0.258) and TF-IDF (0.261). Full per-prefix results and per-prefix error analysis in Appendix~\\ref{sec:actorattack_full} and~\\ref{app:aa_error}.\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal modeling.}\nMean pooling matches BiGRU on both DD and ActorAttack (F1 gap $\\leq$ 1\\,pp, overlapping CIs; Appendix~\\ref{app:temporal}).\nTurn-order shuffling produces negligible change ($|\\Delta| < 1$\\,pp) across both DeBERTa-v3-base 9-class (DD $\\Delta$\\,=\\,0.000, AA $\\Delta$\\,=\\,$-$0.009, IID $\\Delta$\\,=\\,0.000) and RoBERTa-large 9-class (DD $\\Delta$\\,=\\,+0.001, AA $\\Delta$\\,=\\,$-$0.007, IID $\\Delta$\\,=\\,0.000), confirming that detection relies on per-turn representations rather than sequential patterns.\n\n\\paragraph{Summary.}\nOn synthetic multi-turn data, domain exposure is the primary mechanism; domain-relevant classification objectives suffice for template attacks ($d < 0.1$ on DD/FITD) with a difficulty gradient on ActorAttack ($d$\\,=\\,0.44); label granularity, temporal modeling, and domain specificity are non-factors. Classification supervision shows a directional variance pattern ($n$\\,=\\,3--5, descriptive). User-turn distributions overlap substantially between benign and jailbreak conversations (IID 8.7$\\pm$2.1 vs.\\ 7.9$\\pm$1.5; DD 8.1$\\pm$1.5 vs.\\ 7.9$\\pm$1.5; FITD 7.8$\\pm$1.4 vs.\\ 7.9$\\pm$1.5; only AA partially separated: 5.1$\\pm$1.7 vs.\\ 7.9$\\pm$1.5); a turn-count-only baseline achieves F1\\,=\\,0.42--0.56 on IID/DD/FITD and 0.00 on AA/ToxicChat, confirming content-driven detection (Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}). Detection near-saturates at 50 conversations (Appendix~\\ref{app:data_efficiency}) and is insensitive to auxiliary loss weight (Appendix~\\ref{app:lambda}).\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------",
      "numLines": 90,
      "startLine": 55,
      "totalLines": 224
    }

```text
1	% conclusion.tex — D073 Phase 2 revision (page-compressed)
2	
3	\section{Discussion}
4	\label{sec:discussion}
5	
6	\paragraph{Confounds and transferability.}
7	Three observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\,=\,0.541$\pm$0.207), scrambled labels within 3.9\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\,$\geq$\,0.97). ToxicChat (+18--42\,pp; Table~\ref{tab:human_data}) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer; mixed-LLM 9-class maintains DD F1\,=\,0.818$\pm$0.096 while MLM collapses (Appendix~\ref{app:mixed_llm}).
8	
9	\paragraph{Evidence consistent with discriminative compression.}
10	Classification DAPT is associated with feature compression into few dimensions (participation ratio 3.7--9.4 vs.\ 25--32; Fisher 2.6--19.7 vs.\ $<$0.001; Appendix~\ref{app:representation}). Scrambled labels yield comparable compression (Fisher\,=\,2.6), consistent with \citet{zhang2021understanding}, with two findings: (i)~domain-relevant partitioning is necessary (topic DAPT has higher per-turn Fisher yet fails at conversation level; Appendix~\ref{app:topic_failure}), and (ii)~compression localizes to 3 SVD dimensions in layers 8--12 (Appendix~\ref{app:layerwise}). A discriminative probe over these 3 dimensions achieves perfect AA OOD detection (sufficiency) and their removal degrades DD by 17\,pp while FITD is unaffected (partial necessity; Appendix~\ref{app:discriminative_probe}); frozen probing near-parity (3.1\,pp) is consistent with this interpretation.
11	
12	\paragraph{Condition-specific advantage and boundaries.}
13	The hierarchy is specific to synthetic multi-turn evaluation. On ToxicChat, JB-MLM leads (0.837) while 9-class ranks lower (0.596); 65\% of the gap stems from false positives (FPR 0.510 vs.\ 0.167), confirmed as high-confidence misalignment (Appendix~\ref{app:confidence}); truncation widens the 9-class advantage (+6.0\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:truncated}). DAPT utility is scale-dependent: at $\geq$355M, vanilla suffices; mean pooling matches BiGRU ($\leq$1\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:temporal}). Benign-side shift (WildChat FPR 48.1\% (vanilla) to 68.1\% (9-class)) is mitigated by 10 conversations (FPR$\rightarrow$0\%; OASST2 1.0\%$\pm$0.5\%, $N$\,=\,25; Appendix~\ref{app:oasst2}).
14	
15	\section{Conclusion}
16	
17	On synthetic multi-turn data, thirteen encoders decompose jailbreak generalization into attack-side (discriminative compression; MLM collapses under mixed-LLM) and benign-side (FPR 58--68\%$\rightarrow$0--1.5\%, $N$\,$\leq$\,25) axes at 24.3$\times$ speedup; classification suffices for template attacks ($d\!<\!0.1$; AA $d$\,=\,0.44) at base scale; the hierarchy reverses on human data, confirming condition specificity.
18	
19	\section{Limitations}
20	\label{sec:limitations}
21	
22	All findings derive from synthetic conversations generated by a single LLM (Qwen3-8B; 350 training, 76--118 test per family, 3 seeds); the evidence hierarchy reverses on human single-turn data (Section~\ref{sec:discussion}), and no human-generated multi-turn jailbreak dataset exists. OOD F1 figures are upper bounds because benign data is IID; estimated dual-OOD F1 drops 12--14\,pp without adaptation but recovers within 0.3\,pp after few-shot adaptation (Appendix~\ref{app:dual_ood}). Benign-side adaptation (WildChat FPR 68.1\%\,$\rightarrow$\,0\% with 10 conversations; OASST2 57.5\%\,$\rightarrow$\,1.0\% at $N$\,=\,25) is validated on only two platforms with small samples ($n \leq 49$). Benign-only adaptation does not address cross-LLM distribution shift: adapting on Llama-origin conversations collapses Llama jailbreak detection (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}). Only persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope; single-turn token-level attacks~\citep{zou2023gcg} and non-text modalities are not addressed.
23	
24	\section*{Ethics Statement}
25	
26	This work is defensive: it improves detection of multi-turn jailbreak attacks rather than developing new attacks. We acknowledge dual-use risk, as the persuasion taxonomy could inform adversarial actors. We mitigate this by using only synthetic conversations (no real harmful content) and withholding attack-generation prompts. Detection code, model weights, and evaluation data will be released under CC BY-NC 4.0. No human subjects were involved.
27	
```
